Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

read · completed

Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/ponytail.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.\n\n(End of file - total 36 lines)\n</content>",

        "text": "---\ndescription: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.\nglobs:\nalwaysApply: true\n---\n\n# Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode\n\nYou are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.\n\nBefore writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:\n\n1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)\n2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.\n3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.\n4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.\n5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.\n6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.\n7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.\n\nThe ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.\n\nBug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.\n\nRules:\n\n- No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.\n- No new dependency if it can be avoided.\n- No boilerplate nobody asked for.\n- Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.\n- Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.\n- Question complex requests: \"Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?\"\n- Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.\n- Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.\n\nNot lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.",

10:  
11: +### Configuration check
12: +
13: +FRR skips any line of `frr.conf` it cannot parse and starts with the rest of the file, so a typo would leave a node running with part of its configuration missing and the mistake visible only in its log. To catch this, the deploy checks each node's `frr.conf` with `vtysh --dryrun` and fails if the file does not parse, quoting each line FRR would skip:
14: +
15: +```
16: +deployment failed for one or more nodes: node "router1" post-deploy:
17: +clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:
18: +  line 5: % Unknown command[21]:  ip adress 10.0.0.1/32
19: +It is the lab's copy of router1/frr.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.
20: +```
21: +
22: +The node that failed the check is left running, with its ssh keys and password in place, so it can be inspected.
23: +
24: +The file checked is the lab directory's copy, which is the one the node uses. Created from `startup-config` on the first deploy, it is kept by later deploys unless [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config) is set, which is why a fixed `startup-config` needs `--reconfigure` to reach the node.
25: +
26: +The check covers what does not parse: unknown commands, invalid values and incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon then rejects, such as a BGP neighbor assigned to a peer-group that does not exist, still deploys, as does configuration for a daemon that is not enabled in [`daemons`](#daemons). FRR picks up the container's hostname, which containerlab already sets to the node name.\n \n+### Configuration check\n+\n+FRR skips any line of `frr.conf` it cannot parse and starts with the rest of the file, so a typo would leave a node running with part of its configuration missing and the mistake visible only in its log. To catch this, the deploy checks each node's `frr.conf` with `vtysh --dryrun` and fails if the file does not parse, quoting each line FRR would skip:\n+\n+```\n+deployment failed for one or more nodes: node \"router1\" post-deploy:\n+clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:\n+  line 5: % Unknown command[21]:  ip adress 10.0.0.1/32\n+It is the lab's copy of router1/frr.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.\n+```\n+\n+The node that failed the check is left running, with its ssh keys and password in place, so it can be inspected.\n+\n+The file checked is the lab directory's copy, which is the one the node uses. Created from `startup-config` on the first deploy, it is kept by later deploys unless [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config) is set, which is why a fixed `startup-config` needs `--reconfigure` to reach the node.\n+\n+The check covers what does not parse: unknown commands, invalid values and incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon then rejects, such as a BGP neighbor assigned to a peer-group that does not exist, still deploys, as does configuration for a daemon that is not enabled in [`daemons`](#daemons). Look for `processing failure` in `docker logs <node>` to find the first kind.\n+\n ### Daemons\n \n By default all daemons supported by this kind are enabled. 41: 	// authzKeysTargets are the "user:home" pairs whose authorized_keys file is
42: 	// populated: root for a shell and admin for vtysh, both reached with the
43: 	// same key.
44: 	authzKeysTargets = "root:/root admin:/home/admin"
45: 
46: 	// authzKeysGuard skips a user the image does not have. admin ships only in
47: 	// the containerlab flavour, so a plain release image still gets root's keys
48: 	// instead of an error.
49: 	authzKeysGuard = `id -u "$user" >/dev/null 2>&1 || continue`
50: 
51: 	// authzKeysChown hands the file to the user who logs in with it; sshd
52: 	// rejects an authorized_keys that user does not own.
53: 	authzKeysChown = `chown -R "$user:" "$home/.ssh"`
54: 
55: 	// authzKeysScript writes the public keys, passed to it as $1, to the
56: 	// authorized_keys of every user in authzKeysTargets that exists.
57: 	authzKeysScript = `set -e
58: for entry in ` + authzKeysTargets + `; do
59: 	user=${entry%%:*}
60: 	home=${entry#*:}
61: 
62: 	` + authzKeysGuard + `
63: 
64: 	mkdir -p "$home/.ssh"
65: 	chmod 700 "$home/.ssh"
66: 	# The file is removed rather than truncated: a redirect onto an existing
67: 	# file keeps that file's ownership, and sshd rejects an authorized_keys
68: 	# that its user does not own.
69: 	rm -f "$home/.ssh/authorized_keys"
70: 	printf '%s\n' "$1" > "$home/.ssh/authorized_keys"
71: 	chmod 600 "$home/.ssh/authorized_keys"
72: 	` + authzKeysChown + `
73: done`
74: 
75: 	// passwordGuard skips a user the image does not have. A plain release image
76: 	// has no admin, and root there is still reachable with the host's keys.
77: 	passwordGuard = `id -u "$1" >/dev/null 2>&1 || exit 0`
78: 
79: 	// passwordScript sets the password of the user named by $1 to $2. Both
80: 	// travel as arguments rather than in the script text, so no character in
81: 	// the password can be read as shell.
82: 	passwordScript = passwordGuard + `
83: printf '%s:%s\n' "$1" "$2" | chpasswd`
84: 
85: 	// watchfrr invokes vtysh -w for CLI saves. Repair the bind-mounted file
86: 	// before returning, while ordinary vtysh sessions still exec the binary.

88: case "$*" in
89:     -w|--writeconfig)
90:         "/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab" "$@" || exit $?
91:         chown %d:%d "/etc/frr/frr.conf" || exit $?

156: func (n *frr) Init(cfg *clabtypes.NodeConfig, opts ...clabnodes.NodeOption) error {
157: 	// Init DefaultNode
158: 	n.DefaultNode = *clabnodes.NewDefaultNode(n)
159: 
160: 	n.Cfg = cfg
161: 	for _, o := range opts {
162: 		o(n)
163: 	}
164: 
165: 	// The whole directory is mounted rather than the three files individually.
166: 	// FRR saves a configuration by renaming frr.conf to frr.conf.sav and
167: 	// writing a new one, and a single-file bind mount cannot be renamed, so
168: 	// per-file mounts make every "write memory" report
169: 	//
170: 	//	Error renaming /etc/frr/frr.conf to /etc/frr/frr.conf.sav: Resource busy
171: 	//
172: 	// and lose the backup. Mounting the directory costs nothing: the image
173: 	// ships only daemons in /etc/frr, and containerlab writes that file too.
174: 	n.Cfg.Binds = append(n.Cfg.Binds,
175: 		fmt.Sprint(filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir), ":", etcFRR),
176: 	)
177: 
178: 	// FRR programs routes into the kernel, which only forwards if asked to.
179: 	n.Cfg.Sysctls["net.ipv4.ip_forward"] = "1"
180: 	n.Cfg.Sysctls["net.ipv6.conf.all.forwarding"] = "1"
181: 
182: 	return nil
183: }

185: func (n *frr) PreDeploy(_ context.Context, params *clabnodes.PreDeployParams) error {
186: 	clabutils.CreateDirectory(n.Cfg.LabDir, clabconstants.PermissionsOpen)
187: 
188: 	_, err := n.LoadOrGenerateCertificate(params.Cert, params.TopologyName)
189: 	if err != nil {
190: 		return err
191: 	}
192: 
193: 	// Recorded here and written to the container in PostDeploy, once it runs.
194: 	n.sshPubKeys = params.SSHPubKeys
195: 
196: 	return n.createFRRFiles()
197: }

199: func (n *frr) createFRRFiles() error {
200: 	nodeCfg := n.Config()
201: 
202: 	dir := filepath.Join(nodeCfg.LabDir, cfgDir)
203: 	clabutils.CreateDirectory(dir, clabconstants.PermissionsOpen)
204: 
205: 	// frr.conf comes from the user's startup-config when given, and from the
206: 	// bundled template otherwise. Routing it through GenerateConfig is what
207: 	// makes enforce-startup-config, suppress-startup-config and templating
208: 	// behave the same way here as for every other kind.
209: 	cfgTemplate := defaultCfgTemplate
210: 
211: 	if nodeCfg.StartupConfig != "" {
212: 		c, err := os.ReadFile(nodeCfg.StartupConfig)
213: 		if err != nil {
214: 			return err
215: 		}
216: 
217: 		cfgTemplate = string(c)
218: 	}
219: 
220: 	confPath := filepath.Join(dir, frrConfFile)
221: 
222: 	err := n.GenerateConfig(confPath, cfgTemplate)
223: 	if err != nil {
224: 		return fmt.Errorf("node=%s, failed to generate config: %w", nodeCfg.ShortName, err)
225: 	}
226: 
227: 	// GenerateConfig keeps an existing frr.conf, which may be one a previous
228: 	// "write memory" left owned by frr and unreadable, so repair it here too
229: 	// rather than only on save.
230: 	err = normalizeConfigFile(confPath)
231: 	if err != nil {
232: 		return fmt.Errorf("node=%s: %w", nodeCfg.ShortName, err)
233: 	}
234: 
235: 	rendered, err := renderDaemons(n.kindSpecificCfg().Daemons)
236: 	if err != nil {
237: 		return fmt.Errorf("node=%s: %w", nodeCfg.ShortName, err)
238: 	}
239: 
240: 	err = clabutils.CreateFile(filepath.Join(dir, daemonsFile), rendered)
241: 	if err != nil {
242: 		return fmt.Errorf("node=%s, failed to write daemons file: %w", nodeCfg.ShortName, err)
243: 	}
244: 
245: 	err = clabutils.CreateFile(filepath.Join(dir, vtyshConfFile), vtyshCfg)
246: 	if err != nil {
247: 		return fmt.Errorf("node=%s, failed to write vtysh.conf: %w", nodeCfg.ShortName, err)
248: 	}
249: 
250: 	return nil
251: }

253: // PostDeploy installs login credentials and keeps CLI-saved configs readable.
254: func (n *frr) PostDeploy(ctx context.Context, _ *clabnodes.PostDeployParams) error {
255: 	log.Debugf("Running postdeploy actions for frr %q node", n.Cfg.ShortName)
256: 
257: 	err := n.addSSHKeys(ctx)
258: 	if err != nil {
259: 		return err
260: 	}
261: 
262: 	err = n.setPassword(ctx)
263: 	if err != nil {
264: 		return err
265: 	}
266: 
267: 	err = n.installConfigSaveWrapper(ctx)
268: 	if err != nil {
269: 		return err
270: 	}
271: 
272: 	// Last, so that a node with a broken configuration can still be logged
273: 	// into to look at it.
274: 	return n.checkConfig(ctx)
275: }
276: 
277: // checkConfig fails the deploy when the node's frr.conf has lines FRR cannot
278: // parse. FRR skips such a line, applies the rest of the file and keeps
279: // running, and says so only in its log, so a typo would otherwise deploy
280: // successfully with part of the configuration missing. vtysh's dry-run mode
281: // parses the file without applying it, so it needs no running daemons.
282: //
283: // It catches what vtysh itself rejects: unknown commands, bad values and
284: // incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon refuses, or
285: // configuration for a daemon that is not enabled, still goes unnoticed.
286: func (n *frr) checkConfig(ctx context.Context) error {
287: 	confPath := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir, frrConfFile)
288: 
289: 	// suppress-startup-config leaves no frr.conf to check.
290: 	if !clabutils.FileExists(confPath) {
291: 		return nil
292: 	}
293: 
294: 	cmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(
295: 		[]string{"vtysh", "--dryrun", "--inputfile", filepath.Join(etcFRR, frrConfFile)})
296: 
297: 	result, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
298: 	if err != nil {
299: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to check the FRR configuration: %w", err)
300: 	}
301: 
302: 	if result.GetReturnCode() == 0 {
303: 		return nil
304: 	}
305: 
306: 	return fmt.Errorf(
307: 		"%s has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:\n%s\n%s",
308: 		confPath, configCheckErrors(result), n.configFixHint())
309: }
310: 
311: // configCheckErrors returns the lines of a failed dry run that name a line of
312: // frr.conf, or all of its output if none does.
313: func configCheckErrors(result *clabexec.ExecResult) string {
314: 	out := strings.TrimSpace(result.GetStdOutString() + "\n" + result.GetStdErrString())
315: 
316: 	var errs []string
317: 
318: 	for l := range strings.SplitSeq(out, "\n") {
319: 		if configErrorLine.MatchString(l) {
320: 			errs = append(errs, "  "+strings.TrimSpace(l))
321: 		}
322: 	}
323: 
324: 	if len(errs) == 0 {
325: 		return "  " + strings.ReplaceAll(out, "\n", "\n  ")
326: 	}
327: 
328: 	return strings.Join(errs, "\n")
329: }
330: 
331: // configFixHint says where to fix the configuration. Where it came from a
332: // startup-config, the lab directory holds a copy that later deploys keep, so
333: // fixing the startup-config alone would not reach the node.
334: func (n *frr) configFixHint() string {
335: 	switch {
336: 	case n.Cfg.StartupConfig == "":
337: 		return "Fix it, then destroy the lab without --cleanup and deploy again, " +
338: 			"which keeps the file."
339: 	case n.Cfg.EnforceStartupConfig:
340: 		return fmt.Sprintf("It is copied from %s on every deploy: fix that file and deploy again.",
341: 			n.Cfg.StartupConfig)
342: 	default:
343: 		return fmt.Sprintf("It is the lab's copy of %s, which later deploys keep: "+
344: 			"fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.", n.Cfg.StartupConfig)
345: 	}
346: }
347: 
348: func (n *frr) installConfigSaveWrapper(ctx context.Context) error {
349: 	uid, gid, err := clabutils.GetRealUserIDs()
350: 	if err != nil {
351: 		return err
352: 	}
353: 
354: 	// FRR can execute vtysh during PostDeploy. A same-directory hard link keeps
355: 	// the original available and preserves its metadata, including symlink identity.
356: 	// Publish the wrapper atomically only after the backup exists.
357: 	// Preserve the original backup across sequential PostDeploy calls.
358: 	script := `set -e
359: wrapper_tmp=''
360: trap 'rm -f -- "$wrapper_tmp"' EXIT
361: if [ ! -f "/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab" ]; then
362:     ln -T -- "/usr/bin/vtysh" "/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab"
363: fi
364: wrapper_tmp=$(mktemp "/usr/bin/vtysh.wrapper.XXXXXX")
365: printf '%s\n' "$1" > "$wrapper_tmp"
366: chmod 755 "$wrapper_tmp"
367: mv -f -- "$wrapper_tmp" "/usr/bin/vtysh"`
368: 
369: 	wrapper := fmt.Sprintf(configSaveWrapper, uid, gid, clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)
370: 	cmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice([]string{"bash", "-c", script, "--", wrapper})
371: 
372: 	result, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
373: 	if err != nil {
374: 		return fmt.Errorf(
375: 			"failed to install config save wrapper on node %q: %w",
376: 			n.Cfg.ShortName,
377: 			err,
378: 		)
379: 	}
380: 	if result.GetReturnCode() != 0 {
381: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to install config save wrapper on node %q: %s",
382: 			n.Cfg.ShortName, result.GetStdErrString())
383: 	}
384: 
385: 	return nil
386: }
387: 
388: // addSSHKeys adds the public keys containerlab collected from the host to the
389: // root and admin users' authorized_keys, to enable passwordless ssh. root gets
390: // a shell and admin gets vtysh, and both are reached with the same key.
391: func (n *frr) addSSHKeys(ctx context.Context) error {
392: 	if len(n.sshPubKeys) == 0 {
393: 		return nil
394: 	}
395: 
396: 	keys := strings.Join(clabutils.MarshalSSHPubKeys(n.sshPubKeys), "\n")
397: 
398: 	cmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(
399: 		[]string{"bash", "-c", authzKeysScript, "--", keys})
400: 
401: 	execResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
402: 	if err != nil {
403: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to add ssh keys to node %q: %w", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)
404: 	}
405: 
406: 	if execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {
407: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to add ssh keys to node %q: %s",
408: 			n.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())
409: 	}
410: 
411: 	return nil
412: }
413: 
414: // setPassword sets the password of the node's credentials user: admin with
415: // password admin, unless the topology's credentials say otherwise. The image
416: // ships admin without a password, so a well-known one is not baked into it;
417: // this is what makes the kind's default, or a topology's own credentials, work
418: // for a password login.
419: func (n *frr) setPassword(ctx context.Context) error {
420: 	user := n.Cfg.Credentials.Username
421: 	password := n.Cfg.Credentials.Password
422: 
423: 	if user == "" || password == "" {
424: 		return nil
425: 	}
426: 
427: 	// chpasswd reads one user:password pair per line, so a line break in the
428: 	// password would start a second pair, for whichever user it names.
429: 	if strings.ContainsAny(password, "\r\n") {
430: 		return fmt.Errorf("node %q: password must not contain a line break", n.Cfg.ShortName)
431: 	}
432: 
433: 	cmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(
434: 		[]string{"bash", "-c", passwordScript, "--", user, password})
435: 
436: 	execResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
437: 	if err != nil {
438: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to set password on node %q: %w", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)
439: 	}
440: 
441: 	if execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {
442: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to set password on node %q: %s",
443: 			n.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())
444: 	}
445: 
446: 	return nil
447: }
448: 
449: // normalizeConfigFile hands the node's frr.conf back to the user who ran
450: // containerlab. FRR writes that file as frr:frr mode 0600 whenever the node is
451: // asked to "write memory", and it is bind mounted, so the ownership lands on
452: // the lab directory copy and leaves the user unable to read their own saved
453: // configuration without root. Every other file containerlab puts in a lab
454: // directory goes through CreateFile, which does the same thing.
455: func normalizeConfigFile(path string) error {
456: 	// Nothing to fix up when the config was suppressed and never written.
457: 	if !clabutils.FileExists(path) {
458: 		return nil
459: 	}
460: 
461: 	err := clabutils.SetUIDAndGID(path)
462: 	if err != nil {
463: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to set ownership of %s: %w", path, err)
464: 	}
465: 
466: 	// Both os.WriteFile and os.Create apply a mode only when they create the
467: 	// file, so a 0600 that FRR left behind survives the write and has to be
468: 	// reset explicitly.
469: 	err = os.Chmod(path, clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)
470: 	if err != nil {
471: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to set permissions of %s: %w", path, err)
472: 	}
473: 
474: 	return nil
475: }
476: 
477: func (n *frr) SaveConfig(ctx context.Context) (*clabnodes.SaveConfigResult, error) {
478: 	cmd, _ := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromString(saveCmd)
479: 
480: 	execResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
481: 	if err != nil {
482: 		return nil, err
483: 	}
484: 
485: 	if execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {
486: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to save config on node %q: %s",
487: 			n.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())
488: 	}
489: 
490: 	confPath := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir, frrConfFile)
491: 
492: 	err = os.WriteFile(confPath, execResult.GetStdOutByteSlice(),
493: 		clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)
494: 	if err != nil {
495: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to write config by %s path from %s container: %w",
496: 			confPath, n.Cfg.ShortName, err)
497: 	}
498: 
499: 	err = normalizeConfigFile(confPath)
500: 	if err != nil {
501: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("node=%s: %w", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)
502: 	}
503: 
504: 	log.Infof("saved FRR configuration from %s node to %s\n", n.Cfg.ShortName, confPath)
505: 
506: 	// Reporting the path is what makes "containerlab save --copy" pick the file
507: 	// up. A nil result is how a kind says it cannot save at all, and copying is
508: 	// skipped for it without an error, so returning one here loses the config
509: 	// quietly.
510: 	return &clabnodes.SaveConfigResult{
511: 		ConfigPath: confPath,
512: 	}, nil
513: }

sshd\n\t// rejects an authorized_keys that user does not own.\n\tauthzKeysChown = `chown -R \"$user:\" \"$home/.ssh\"`\n\n\t// authzKeysScript writes the public keys, passed to it as $1, to the\n\t// authorized_keys of every user in authzKeysTargets that exists.\n\tauthzKeysScript = `set -e\nfor entry in ` + authzKeysTargets + `; do\n\tuser=${entry%%:*}\n\thome=${entry#*:}\n\n\t` + authzKeysGuard + `\n\n\tmkdir -p \"$home/.ssh\"\n\tchmod 700 \"$home/.ssh\"\n\t# The file is removed rather than truncated: a redirect onto an existing\n\t# file keeps that file's ownership, and sshd rejects an authorized_keys\n\t# that its user does not own.\n\trm -f \"$home/.ssh/authorized_keys\"\n\tprintf '%s\\n' \"$1\" > \"$home/.ssh/authorized_keys\"\n\tchmod 600 \"$home/.ssh/authorized_keys\"\n\t` + authzKeysChown + `\ndone`\n\n\t// passwordGuard skips a user the image does not have. Both\n\t// travel as arguments rather than in the script text, so no character in\n\t// the password can be read as shell.\n\tpasswordScript = passwordGuard + `\nprintf '%s:%s\\n' \"$1\" \"$2\" | chpasswd`\n\n\t// watchfrr invokes vtysh -w for CLI saves. Repair the bind-mounted file\n\t// before returning, while ordinary vtysh sessions still exec the binary.\n\tconfigSaveWrapper = `#!/bin/sh\ncase \"$*\" in\n    -w|--writeconfig)\n        \"/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab\" \"$@\" || exit $?\n        chown %d:%d \"/etc/frr/frr.conf\" || exit $?\n        chmod %04o \"/etc/frr/frr.conf\"\n        ;;\n    *)\n        exec \"/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab\" \"$@\"\n        ;;\nesac`\n\n\t// no-header keeps the \"Building configuration...\" preamble out of the\n\t// saved file, which is written back as the node's frr.conf.\n\tsaveCmd = `vtysh -c \"show running-config no-header\"`\n)\n\nvar (\n\t// configErrorLine matches the lines in which vtysh names a line of the\n\t// configuration it could not parse, e.g. \"line 5: % Unknown command[21]: ...\".\n\tconfigErrorLine = regexp.MustCompile(`^\\s*line \\d+:`)\n\n\t// defaultCredentials is the admin user the containerlab image ships, whose\n\t// login shell is vtysh. CLI-saved configs readable.\nfunc (n *frr) PostDeploy(ctx context.Context, _ *clabnodes.PostDeployParams) error {\n\tlog.Debugf(\"Running postdeploy actions for frr %q node\", n.Cfg.ShortName)\n\n\terr := n.addSSHKeys(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\n\terr = n.setPassword(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\n\terr = FRR skips such a line, applies the rest of the file and keeps\n// running, and says so only in its log, so a typo would otherwise deploy\n// successfully with part of the configuration missing. vtysh's dry-run mode\n// parses the file without applying it, so it needs no running daemons.\n//\n// It catches what vtysh itself rejects: unknown commands, bad values and\n// incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon refuses, or\n// configuration for a daemon that is not enabled, still goes unnoticed.\nfunc (n *frr) checkConfig(ctx context.Context) error {\n\tconfPath := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir, frrConfFile)\n\n\t// suppress-startup-config leaves no frr.conf to check.\n\tif !clabutils.FileExists(confPath) {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\tcmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(\n\t\t[]string{\"vtysh\", \"--dryrun\", \"--inputfile\", filepath.Join(etcFRR, frrConfFile)})\n\n\tresult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to check the FRR configuration: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\n\tif result.GetReturnCode() == 0 {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\treturn fmt.Errorf(\n\t\t\"%s has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:\\n%s\\n%s\",\n\t\tconfPath, configCheckErrors(result), n.configFixHint())\n}\n\n// configCheckErrors returns the lines of a failed dry run that name a line of\n// frr.conf, or all of its output if none does.\nfunc configCheckErrors(result *clabexec.ExecResult) string {\n\tout := strings.TrimSpace(result.GetStdOutString() + \"\\n\" + result.GetStdErrString())\n\n\tvar errs []string\n\n\tfor l := range strings.SplitSeq(out, \"\\n\") {\n\t\tif configErrorLine.MatchString(l) {\n\t\t\terrs = append(errs, \"  \"+strings.TrimSpace(l))\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tif len(errs) == 0 {\n\t\treturn \"  \" + strings.ReplaceAll(out, \"\\n\", \"\\n  \")\n\t}\n\n\treturn strings.Join(errs, \"\\n\")\n}\n\n// configFixHint says where to fix the configuration. Where it came from a\n// startup-config, the lab directory holds a copy that later deploys keep, so\n// fixing the startup-config alone would not reach the node.\nfunc (n *frr) configFixHint() string {\n\tswitch {\n\tcase n.Cfg.StartupConfig == \"\":\n\t\treturn \"Fix it, then destroy the lab without --cleanup and deploy again, \" +\n\t\t\t\"which keeps the file.\"\n\tcase n.Cfg.EnforceStartupConfig:\n\t\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"It is copied from %s on every deploy: fix that file and deploy again.\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.StartupConfig)\n\tdefault:\n\t\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"It is the lab's copy of %s, which later deploys keep: \"+\n\t\t\t\"fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.\", n.Cfg.StartupConfig)\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (n *frr) installConfigSaveWrapper(ctx context.Context) error {\n\tuid, gid, err := clabutils.GetRealUserIDs()\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\n\t// FRR can execute vtysh during PostDeploy. A same-directory hard link keeps\n\t// the original available and preserves its metadata, including symlink identity.\n\t// Publish the wrapper atomically only after the backup exists.\n\t// Preserve the original backup across sequential PostDeploy calls.\n\tscript := `set -e\nwrapper_tmp=''\ntrap 'rm -f -- \"$wrapper_tmp\"' EXIT\nif [ ! -f \"/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab\" ]; then\n    ln -T -- \"/usr/bin/vtysh\" \"/usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab\"\nfi\nwrapper_tmp=$(mktemp \"/usr/bin/vtysh.wrapper.XXXXXX\")\nprintf '%s\\n' \"$1\" > \"$wrapper_tmp\"\nchmod 755 \"$wrapper_tmp\"\nmv -f -- \"$wrapper_tmp\" \"/usr/bin/vtysh\"`\n\n\twrapper := fmt.Sprintf(configSaveWrapper, uid, gid, clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)\n\tcmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice([]string{\"bash\", \"-c\", script, \"--\", wrapper})\n\n\tresult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\n\t\t\t\"failed to install config save wrapper on node %q: %w\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.ShortName,\n\t\t\terr,\n\t\t)\n\t}\n\tif result.GetReturnCode() != 0 {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to install config save wrapper on node %q: %s\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.ShortName, result.GetStdErrString())\n\t}\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// addSSHKeys adds the public keys containerlab collected from the host to the\n// root and admin users' authorized_keys, to enable passwordless ssh. root gets\n// a shell and admin gets vtysh, and both are reached with the same key.\nfunc (n *frr) addSSHKeys(ctx context.Context) error {\n\tif len(n.sshPubKeys) == 0 {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\tkeys := strings.Join(clabutils.MarshalSSHPubKeys(n.sshPubKeys), \"\\n\")\n\n\tcmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(\n\t\t[]string{\"bash\", \"-c\", authzKeysScript, \"--\", keys})\n\n\texecResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to add ssh keys to node %q: %w\", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)\n\t}\n\n\tif execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to add ssh keys to node %q: %s\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())\n\t}\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// setPassword sets the password of the node's credentials user: admin with\n// password admin, unless the topology's credentials say otherwise. The image\n// ships admin without a password, so a well-known one is not baked into it;\n// this is what makes the kind's default, or a topology's own credentials, work\n// for a password login.\nfunc (n *frr) setPassword(ctx context.Context) error {\n\tuser := n.Cfg.Credentials.Username\n\tpassword := n.Cfg.Credentials.Password\n\n\tif user == \"\" || password == \"\" {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\t// chpasswd reads one user:password pair per line, so a line break in the\n\t// password would start a second pair, for whichever user it names.\n\tif strings.ContainsAny(password, \"\\r\\n\") {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"node %q: password must not contain a line break\", n.Cfg.ShortName)\n\t}\n\n\tcmd := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromSlice(\n\t\t[]string{\"bash\", \"-c\", passwordScript, \"--\", user, password})\n\n\texecResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to set password on node %q: %w\", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)\n\t}\n\n\tif execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to set password on node %q: %s\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())\n\t}\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// normalizeConfigFile hands the node's frr.conf back to the user who ran\n// containerlab. FRR writes that file as frr:frr mode 0600 whenever the node is\n// asked to \"write memory\", and it is bind mounted, so the ownership lands on\n// the lab directory copy and leaves the user unable to read their own saved\n// configuration without root. Every other file containerlab puts in a lab\n// directory goes through CreateFile, which does the same thing.\nfunc normalizeConfigFile(path string) error {\n\t// Nothing to fix up when the config was suppressed and never written.\n\tif !clabutils.FileExists(path) {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\terr := clabutils.SetUIDAndGID(path)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to set ownership of %s: %w\", path, err)\n\t}\n\n\t// Both os.WriteFile and os.Create apply a mode only when they create the\n\t// file, so a 0600 that FRR left behind survives the write and has to be\n\t// reset explicitly.\n\terr = os.Chmod(path, clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to set permissions of %s: %w\", path, err)\n\t}\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\nfunc (n *frr) SaveConfig(ctx context.Context) (*clabnodes.SaveConfigResult, error) {\n\tcmd, _ := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromString(saveCmd)\n\n\texecResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, err\n\t}\n\n\tif execResult.GetReturnCode() != 0 {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"failed to save config on node %q: %s\",\n\t\t\tn.Cfg.ShortName, execResult.GetStdErrString())\n\t}\n\n\tconfPath := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir, frrConfFile)\n\n\terr = os.WriteFile(confPath, execResult.GetStdOutByteSlice(),\n\t\tclabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"failed to write config by %s path from %s container: %w\",\n\t\t\tconfPath, n.Cfg.ShortName, err)\n\t}\n\n\terr = normalizeConfigFile(confPath)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"node=%s: %w\", n.Cfg.ShortName, err)\n\t}\n\n\tlog.Infof(\"saved FRR configuration from %s node to %s\\n\", n.Cfg.ShortName, confPath)\n\n\t// Reporting the path is what makes \"containerlab save --copy\" pick the file\n\t// up. A nil result is how a kind says it cannot save at all, and copying is\n\t// skipped for it without an error, so returning one here loses the config\n\t// quietly.\n\treturn &clabnodes.SaveConfigResult{\n\t\tConfigPath: confPath,\n\t}, nil\n}",

Output

<path>/source/head/docs/manual/kinds/frr.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: search:
3:   boost: 4
4: kind_code_name: frr
5: kind_display_name: FRRouting
6: ---
7: # -{{ kind_display_name }}-
8: 
9: [-{{ kind_display_name }}-](https://frrouting.org) is an open source internet routing protocol suite for Linux. It is identified with the `-{{ kind_code_name }}-` kind in the [topology file](../topo-def-file.md). The `frrouting` kind name is accepted as an alias.
10: 
11: ## FRR via the `linux` kind
12: 
13: Earlier containerlab labs ran FRR containers with the [`linux`](linux.md) kind, supplying `frr.conf` and `daemons` through explicit bind mounts. The native `frr` kind builds on that work and makes FRR a first-class citizen in containerlab: it manages configuration files, daemon selection, forwarding, SSH public keys, and saving the running configuration.
14: 
15: To adapt an existing lab, change `kind: linux` to `kind: frr`, replace the bind mount for `/etc/frr/frr.conf` with `startup-config`, and replace the `/etc/frr/daemons` bind mount with the `daemons` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config). Remove any bind for `/etc/frr/vtysh.conf`, since the kind generates it. Use the containerlab image below if you need SSH access. Existing labs can continue using the `linux` kind.
16: 
17: ## Getting -{{ kind_display_name }}- image
18: 
19: FRR publishes a containerlab image alongside its plain release image, tagged `containerlab-<version>` in the same [`quay.io/frrouting/frr`](https://quay.io/repository/frrouting/frr) repository. This image was introduced with **FRR 10.7.1** and is published for 10.7.1 and later releases. **Releases older than 10.7.1 will not receive containerlab-native images.**
20: 
21: ```bash
22: docker pull quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1
23: ```
24: 
25: The containerlab image uses the corresponding FRR release image as its base and adds:
26: 
27: - An OpenSSH server, started alongside `watchfrr`, with SSH host keys generated on first start so each container has its own keys.
28: - An `admin` user whose login shell is `vtysh`, so SSH opens the routing CLI. Its `frr` and `frrvty` group membership lets it configure the router and save configurations. The existing `root` user retains a Linux shell.
29: - An FRR-specific `/etc/motd` describing the two SSH entry points and how to view FRR logs.
30: - A startup script that removes the management network's IPv4 and IPv6 default routes to keep them out of the lab's routing protocols. The connected management routes remain available.
31: 
32: FRR itself and the other base image components are unchanged. The image's build files and README are maintained upstream in [`docker/containerlab`](https://github.com/FRRouting/frr/tree/master/docker/containerlab).
33: 
34: The plain `<version>` tags ship no SSH server. Use `docker exec` to access those containers, as shown in the `vtysh` and `bash` tabs below.
35: 
36: ## Managing -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes
37: 
38: /// tab | SSH
39: The public keys detected on your host are added to both the `admin` and the `root` user, so no password is needed either way.
40: 
41: `admin` is the default user for these nodes, and its login shell is `vtysh`, so a bare `ssh` reaches the routing CLI:
42: 
43: ```bash
44: ssh <node-name>
45: ```
46: 
47: `root` gets a shell:
48: 
49: ```bash
50: ssh root@<node-name>
51: ```
52: 
53: `admin` can also log in with a password, which is `admin` unless the node's [`credentials`](../nodes.md#credentials) set another. The image ships no password for it; containerlab sets this one when it deploys the node, so changing `credentials` changes the password the node accepts. `root` has no password and accepts keys only.
54: 
55: These SSH commands require the containerlab image described above. For plain release images, use the `vtysh` or `bash` tabs below to access them with `docker exec`.
56: ///
57: /// tab | vtysh
58: FRR's integrated shell is available in the container:
59: 
60: ```bash
61: docker exec -it <node-name> vtysh
62: ```
63: 
64: ///
65: /// tab | bash
66: ```bash
67: docker exec -it <node-name> bash
68: ```
69: 
70: ///
71: 
72: ## Interfaces naming
73: 
74: -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes use the `eth` prefix for their data interfaces, so `eth1`, `eth2` and so on. `eth0` is reserved for the management interface.
75: 
76: ## Node configuration
77: 
78: -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes are configured through three files, which containerlab writes into the node's lab directory under `config/`. That directory is bind mounted over the container's `/etc/frr`:
79: 
80: | File | Contents |
81: | --- | --- |
82: | `frr.conf` | the node's running configuration |
83: | `daemons` | which routing daemons to start |
84: | `vtysh.conf` | `service integrated-vtysh-config`, so `frr.conf` is the only config file |
85: 
86: The official image ships none of `frr.conf` and `vtysh.conf`, and `vtysh` refuses to start without them, which is why all three are always written.
87: 
88: Saving a configuration also leaves a `frr.conf.sav` next to them, which is the previous configuration: FRR keeps one backup by renaming the old file before writing the new one.
89: 
90: Files saved with `write memory` or `copy running-config startup-config` belong to the user who deployed the lab and remain readable on the host with `0644` permissions.
91: 
92: ### Startup configuration
93: 
94: Point `startup-config` at an FRR configuration file to have it used as the node's `frr.conf`:
95: 
96: ```yaml
97: topology:
98:   nodes:
99:     router1:
100:       kind: -{{ kind_code_name }}-
101:       image: quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1
102:       startup-config: router1/frr.conf
103: ```
104: 
105: Without a `startup-config` the node comes up with a minimal configuration that only sets `frr defaults traditional` and logging.
106: 
107: The hostname is not set in the generated config on purpose. FRR picks up the container's hostname, which containerlab already sets to the node name.
108: 
109: ### Configuration check
110: 
111: FRR skips any line of `frr.conf` it cannot parse and starts with the rest of the file, so a typo would leave a node running with part of its configuration missing and the mistake visible only in its log. To catch this, the deploy checks each node's `frr.conf` with `vtysh --dryrun` and fails if the file does not parse, quoting each line FRR would skip:
112: 
113: ```
114: deployment failed for one or more nodes: node "router1" post-deploy:
115: clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:
116:   line 5: % Unknown command[21]:  ip adress 10.0.0.1/32
117: It is the lab's copy of router1/frr.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.
118: ```
119: 
120: The node that failed the check is left running, with its ssh keys and password in place, so it can be inspected.
121: 
122: The file checked is the lab directory's copy, which is the one the node uses. Created from `startup-config` on the first deploy, it is kept by later deploys unless [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config) is set, which is why a fixed `startup-config` needs `--reconfigure` to reach the node.
123: 
124: The check covers what does not parse: unknown commands, invalid values and incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon then rejects, such as a BGP neighbor assigned to a peer-group that does not exist, still deploys, as does configuration for a daemon that is not enabled in [`daemons`](#daemons). Look for `processing failure` in `docker logs <node>` to find the first kind.
125: 
126: ### Daemons
127: 
128: By default all daemons supported by this kind are enabled. To run only the daemons a lab actually needs, list them under the `daemons` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config):
129: 
130: ```yaml
131: topology:
132:   nodes:
133:     router1:
134:       kind: -{{ kind_code_name }}-
135:       image: quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1
136:       daemons:
137:         - ospfd
138:         - bfdd
139: ```
140: 
141: Naming any daemon switches off all the ones you did not name. `zebra`, `staticd`, `mgmtd` and `watchfrr` are always started by FRR and may be listed or left out; either way they run.
142: 
143: The list accepts `bgpd`, `ospfd`, `ospf6d`, `ripd`, `ripngd`, `isisd`, `pimd`, `pim6d`, `ldpd`, `nhrpd`, `eigrpd`, `babeld`, `sharpd`, `pbrd`, `bfdd`, `fabricd`, `vrrpd` and `pathd`. Any other name is an error naming the offending entry.
144: 
145: `daemons` can be set on a group or a kind as well as on a single node, so a whole class of routers can share one daemon list. See the [frr01 lab](../../lab-examples/frr01.md) for that.
146: 
147: /// admonition | Daemons and topology size
148:     type: subtle-note
149: Starting all daemons increases the number of processes and memory usage per node. For larger topologies, list only the daemons the lab needs.
150: ///
151: 
152: ### Saving configuration
153: 
154: `containerlab save` writes each node's running configuration back to `config/frr.conf` in its lab directory:
155: 
156: ```bash
157: containerlab save -t <topology-file>
158: ```
159: 
160: The saved file is reused on subsequent deployments while the lab directory exists. To replace it with `startup-config`, set [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config). Destroying the lab with `--cleanup` removes the saved configuration along with the lab directory; copy configurations you want to keep elsewhere first.
161: 
162: ## Host requirements
163: 
164: -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes enable IPv4 and IPv6 forwarding in the container's network namespace, so no host-level sysctl changes are needed.
165: 
166: ## Lab examples
167: 
168: - [FRR OSPF lab](../../lab-examples/frr01.md)

(End of file - total 168 lines)
</content>

The native `frr` kind builds on that work and makes FRR a first-class citizen in containerlab: it manages configuration files, daemon selection, forwarding, SSH public keys, and saving the running configuration.\n\nTo adapt an existing lab, change `kind: linux` to `kind: frr`, replace the bind mount for `/etc/frr/frr.conf` with `startup-config`, and replace the `/etc/frr/daemons` bind mount with the `daemons` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config).         "text": "---\nsearch:\n  boost: 4\nkind_code_name: frr\nkind_display_name: FRRouting\n---\n# -{{ kind_display_name }}-\n\n[-{{ kind_display_name }}-](https://frrouting.org) is an open source internet routing protocol suite for Linux. It is identified with the `-{{ kind_code_name }}-` kind in the [topology file](../topo-def-file.md). The `frrouting` kind name is accepted as an alias.\n\n## FRR via the `linux` kind\n\nEarlier containerlab labs ran FRR containers with the [`linux`](linux.md) kind, supplying `frr.conf` and `daemons` through explicit bind mounts. The native `frr` kind builds on that work and makes FRR a first-class citizen in containerlab: it manages configuration files, daemon selection, forwarding, SSH public keys, and saving the running configuration.\n\nTo adapt an existing lab, change `kind: linux` to `kind: frr`, replace the bind mount for `/etc/frr/frr.conf` with `startup-config`, and replace the `/etc/frr/daemons` bind mount with the `daemons` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config). Remove any bind for `/etc/frr/vtysh.conf`, since the kind generates it. Use the containerlab image below if you need SSH access. Existing labs can continue using the `linux` kind.\n\n## Getting -{{ kind_display_name }}- image\n\nFRR publishes a containerlab image alongside its plain release image, tagged `containerlab-<version>` in the same [`quay.io/frrouting/frr`](https://quay.io/repository/frrouting/frr) repository. This image was introduced with **FRR 10.7.1** and is published for 10.7.1 and later releases. **Releases older than 10.7.1 will not receive containerlab-native images.**\n\n```bash\ndocker pull quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1\n```\n\nThe containerlab image uses the corresponding FRR release image as its base and adds:\n\n- An OpenSSH server, started alongside `watchfrr`, with SSH host keys generated on first start so each container has its own keys.\n- An `admin` user whose login shell is `vtysh`, so SSH opens the routing CLI. Its `frr` and `frrvty` group membership lets it configure the router and save configurations. The existing `root` user retains a Linux shell.\n- An FRR-specific `/etc/motd` describing the two SSH entry points and how to view FRR logs.\n- A startup script that removes the management network's IPv4 and IPv6 default routes to keep them out of the lab's routing protocols. The connected management routes remain available.\n\nFRR itself and the other base image components are unchanged. The image's build files and README are maintained upstream in [`docker/containerlab`](https://github.com/FRRouting/frr/tree/master/docker/containerlab).\n\nThe plain `<version>` tags ship no SSH server. Use `docker exec` to access those containers, as shown in the `vtysh` and `bash` tabs below.\n\n## Managing -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes\n\n/// tab | SSH\nThe public keys detected on your host are added to both the `admin` and the `root` user, so no password is needed either way.\n\n`admin` is the default user for these nodes, and its login shell is `vtysh`, so a bare `ssh` reaches the routing CLI:\n\n```bash\nssh <node-name>\n```\n\n`root` gets a shell:\n\n```bash\nssh root@<node-name>\n```\n\n`admin` can also log in with a password, which is `admin` unless the node's [`credentials`](../nodes.md#credentials) set another. The image ships no password for it; containerlab sets this one when it deploys the node, so changing `credentials` changes the password the node accepts. `root` has no password and accepts keys only.\n\nThese SSH commands require the containerlab image described above. For plain release images, use the `vtysh` or `bash` tabs below to access them with `docker exec`.\n///\n/// tab | vtysh\nFRR's integrated shell is available in the container:\n\n```bash\ndocker exec -it <node-name> vtysh\n```\n\n///\n/// tab | bash\n```bash\ndocker exec -it <node-name> bash\n```\n\n///\n\n## Interfaces naming\n\n-{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes use the `eth` prefix for their data interfaces, so `eth1`, `eth2` and so on. `eth0` is reserved for the management interface.\n\n## Node configuration\n\n-{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes are configured through three files, which containerlab writes into the node's lab directory under `config/`. That directory is bind mounted over the container's `/etc/frr`:\n\n| File | Contents |\n| --- | --- |\n| `frr.conf` | the node's running configuration |\n| `daemons` | which routing daemons to start |\n| `vtysh.conf` | `service integrated-vtysh-config`, so `frr.conf` is the only config file |\n\nThe official image ships none of `frr.conf` and `vtysh.conf`, and `vtysh` refuses to start without them, which is why all three are always written.\n\nSaving a configuration also leaves a `frr.conf.sav` next to them, which is the previous configuration: FRR keeps one backup by renaming the old file before writing the new one.\n\nFiles saved with `write memory` or `copy running-config startup-config` belong to the user who deployed the lab and remain readable on the host with `0644` permissions.\n\n### Startup configuration\n\nPoint `startup-config` at an FRR configuration file to have it used as the node's `frr.conf`:\n\n```yaml\ntopology:\n  nodes:\n    router1:\n      kind: -{{ kind_code_name }}-\n      image: quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1\n      startup-config: router1/frr.conf\n```\n\nWithout a `startup-config` the node comes up with a minimal configuration that only sets `frr defaults traditional` and logging.\n\nThe hostname is not set in the generated config on purpose. FRR picks up the container's hostname, which containerlab already sets to the node name.\n\n### Configuration check\n\nFRR skips any line of `frr.conf` it cannot parse and starts with the rest of the file, so a typo would leave a node running with part of its configuration missing and the mistake visible only in its log. To catch this, the deploy checks each node's `frr.conf` with `vtysh --dryrun` and fails if the file does not parse, quoting each line FRR would skip:\n\n```\ndeployment failed for one or more nodes: node \"router1\" post-deploy:\nclab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf has errors that FRR skips at startup, leaving those lines unconfigured:\n  line 5: % Unknown command[21]:  ip adress 10.0.0.1/32\nIt is the lab's copy of router1/frr.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.\n```\n\nThe node that failed the check is left running, with its ssh keys and password in place, so it can be inspected.\n\nThe file checked is the lab directory's copy, which is the one the node uses. Created from `startup-config` on the first deploy, it is kept by later deploys unless [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config) is set, which is why a fixed `startup-config` needs `--reconfigure` to reach the node.\n\nThe check covers what does not parse: unknown commands, invalid values and incomplete commands. A line that parses but that a daemon then rejects, such as a BGP neighbor assigned to a peer-group that does not exist, still deploys, as does configuration for a daemon that is not enabled in [`daemons`](#daemons). Look for `processing failure` in `docker logs <node>` to find the first kind.\n\n### Daemons\n\nBy default all daemons supported by this kind are enabled. To run only the daemons a lab actually needs, list them under the `daemons` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config):\n\n```yaml\ntopology:\n  nodes:\n    router1:\n      kind: -{{ kind_code_name }}-\n      image: quay.io/frrouting/frr:containerlab-10.7.1\n      daemons:\n        - ospfd\n        - bfdd\n```\n\nNaming any daemon switches off all the ones you did not name. `zebra`, `staticd`, `mgmtd` and `watchfrr` are always started by FRR and may be listed or left out; either way they run.\n\nThe list accepts `bgpd`, `ospfd`, `ospf6d`, `ripd`, `ripngd`, `isisd`, `pimd`, `pim6d`, `ldpd`, `nhrpd`, `eigrpd`, `babeld`, `sharpd`, `pbrd`, `bfdd`, `fabricd`, `vrrpd` and `pathd`. Any other name is an error naming the offending entry.\n\n`daemons` can be set on a group or a kind as well as on a single node, so a whole class of routers can share one daemon list. See the [frr01 lab](../../lab-examples/frr01.md) for that.\n\n/// admonition | Daemons and topology size\n    type: subtle-note\nStarting all daemons increases the number of processes and memory usage per node. For larger topologies, list only the daemons the lab needs.\n///\n\n### Saving configuration\n\n`containerlab save` writes each node's running configuration back to `config/frr.conf` in its lab directory:\n\n```bash\ncontainerlab save -t <topology-file>\n```\n\nThe saved file is reused on subsequent deployments while the lab directory exists. To replace it with `startup-config`, set [`enforce-startup-config`](../nodes.md#enforce-startup-config). Destroying the lab with `--cleanup` removes the saved configuration along with the lab directory; copy configurations you want to keep elsewhere first.\n\n## Host requirements\n\n-{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes enable IPv4 and IPv6 forwarding in the container's network namespace, so no host-level sysctl changes are needed.\n\n## Lab examples\n\n- [FRR OSPF lab](../../lab-examples/frr01.md)",

27: // newTestNode returns an frr node rooted at a temporary lab directory.
28: func newTestNode(t *testing.T, cfg *clabtypes.NodeConfig) *frr {
29: 	t.Helper()
30: 
31: 	cfg.LabDir = filepath.Join(t.TempDir(), cfg.ShortName)
32: 	if cfg.Sysctls == nil {
33: 		cfg.Sysctls = map[string]string{}
34: 	}
35: 
36: 	n := new(frr)
37: 	if err := n.Init(cfg); err != nil {
38: 		t.Fatalf("Init: %v", err)
39: 	}
40: 
41: 	return n
42: }

44: func readConfigFile(t *testing.T, n *frr, name string) string {
45: 	t.Helper()
46: 
47: 	b, err := os.ReadFile(filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir, name))
48: 	if err != nil {
49: 		t.Fatalf("reading %s: %v", name, err)
50: 	}
51: 
52: 	return string(b)
53: }

55: // Init must mount all three config files, since the image ships neither
56: // frr.conf nor vtysh.conf and vtysh will not start without them.
57: // The config directory is mounted as a directory, not as three separate files:
58: // FRR renames frr.conf to frr.conf.sav when saving, and a single-file bind mount
59: // cannot be renamed.
60: func TestInitBindsConfigDirectory(t *testing.T) {

63: 	want := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, cfgDir) + ":" + etcFRR
64: 
65: 	found := false
66:

18: // ParseExecOutputFormat parses the exec output format user input.
19: func ParseExecOutputFormat(s string) (string, error) {
20: 	switch strings.ToLower(strings.TrimSpace(s)) {
21: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
22: 		return clabconstants.FormatJSON, nil
23: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain, clabconstants.FormatTable:

30: // ExecCmd represents an exec command.
31: type ExecCmd struct {
32: 	Cmd []string `json:"cmd"` // Cmd is a slice-based representation of a string command.
33: }
34: 
35: // NewExecCmdFromString creates ExecCmd for a string-based command.
36: func NewExecCmdFromString(cmd string) (*ExecCmd, error) {
37: 	result := &ExecCmd{}
38: 	if err := result.SetCmd(cmd); err != nil {
39: 		return nil, err
40: 	}
41: 	return result, nil
42: }

46: 	return &ExecCmd{

48: 	}
49: }
50: 
51: // Stdout type alias for a string is an artificial type
52: // to allow for custom marshaling of stdout output which can be either
53: // a valid or non valid JSON.
54: // For that reason a custom MarshalJSON method is implemented to take care of both.
55: type Stdout string
56: 
57: // MarshalJSON implements a custom marshaller for a custom Stdout type.
58: func (s Stdout) MarshalJSON() ([]byte, error) {
59: 	switch {
60: 	case json.Valid([]byte(s)):
61: 		return []byte(s), nil
62: 	default:
63: 		return json.Marshal(string(s))
64: 	}
65: }
66: 
67: // ExecResult represents a result of a command execution.
68: type ExecResult struct {
69: 	Cmd        []string `json:"cmd"`
70: 	ReturnCode int      `json:"return-code"`
71: 	Stdout     Stdout   `json:"stdout"`
72: 	Stderr     string   `json:"stderr"`
73: }
74: 
75: func NewExecResult(op *ExecCmd) *ExecResult {
76: 	er := &ExecResult{Cmd: op.GetCmd()}
77: 	return er
78: }
79: 
80: // SetCmd sets the command that is to be executed.
81: func (e *ExecCmd) SetCmd(cmd string) error {
82: 	c, err := shlex.Split(cmd)
83: 	if err != nil {
84: 		return err
85: 	}
86: 	e.Cmd = c
87: 	return nil
88: }
89: 
90: // GetCmd sets the command that is to be executed.
91: func (e *ExecCmd) GetCmd() []string {
92: 	return e.Cmd
93: }
94: 
95: // GetCmdString sets the command that is to be executed.
96: func (e *ExecCmd) GetCmdString() string {
97: 	return strings.Join(e.Cmd, " ")
98: }
99: 
100: func (e *ExecResult) String() string {
101: 	var s strings.Builder
102: 
103: 	s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("Cmd: %s\nReturnCode: %d", e.GetCmdString(), e.ReturnCode))
104: 
105: 	if e.Stdout != "" {
106: 		s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("\nStdout: %q", e.Stdout))
107: 	}
108: 	if e.Stderr != "" {
109: 		s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("\nStderr: %q", e.Stderr))
110: 	}
111: 
112: 	return s.String()
113: }
114: 
115: // Dump dumps execution result as a string in one of the provided formats.
116: func (e *ExecResult) Dump(format string) (string, error) {
117: 	var result string
118: 	switch format {
119: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
120: 		byteData, err := json.MarshalIndent(e, "", "  ")
121: 		if err != nil {
122: 			return "", err
123: 		}
124: 		result = string(byteData)
125: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain:
126: 		result = e.String()
127: 	}
128: 	return result, nil
129: }
130: 
131: // GetCmdString returns the initially parsed cmd as a string for e.g. log output purpose.
132: func (e *ExecResult) GetCmdString() string {
133: 	return strings.Join(e.Cmd, " ")
134: }
135: 
136: func (e *ExecResult) GetReturnCode() int {
137: 	return e.ReturnCode
138: }
139: 
140: func (e *ExecResult) SetReturnCode(rc int) {
141: 	e.ReturnCode = rc
142: }
143: 
144: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdOutString() string {
145: 	return string(e.Stdout)
146: }
147: 
148: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdErrString() string {
149: 	return e.Stderr
150: }
151: 
152: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdOutByteSlice() []byte {
153: 	return []byte(e.Stdout)
154: }
155: 
156: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdErrByteSlice() []byte {
157: 	return []byte(e.Stderr)
158: }
159: 
160: func (e *ExecResult) GetCmd() []string {
161: 	return e.Cmd
162: }
163: 
164: func (e *ExecResult) SetStdOut(data []byte) {
165: 	e.Stdout = Stdout(data)
166: }
167: 
168: func (e *ExecResult) SetStdErr(data []byte) {
169: 	e.Stderr = string(data)
170: }
171: 
172: // execEntries is a map indexed by container IDs storing lists of ExecResult.
173: type execEntries map[string][]*ExecResult
174: 
175: type execEntry struct {
176: 	cId    string
177: 	result *ExecResult
178: }
179: 
180: // ExecCollection represents a datastore for exec commands execution results.
181: type ExecCollection struct {
182: 	execEntries
183: 	orderedEntries []execEntry
184: 	m              sync.RWMutex
185: }
186: 
187: // NewExecCollection initializes the collection of exec command results.
188: func NewExecCollection() *ExecCollection {
189: 	return &ExecCollection{
190: 		execEntries: execEntries{},
191: 		m:           sync.RWMutex{},
192: 	}
193: }
194: 
195: func (ec *ExecCollection) Add(cId string, e *ExecResult) {
196: 	ec.m.Lock()
197: 	defer ec.m.Unlock()
198: 	ec.execEntries[cId] = append(ec.execEntries[cId], e)
199: 	ec.orderedEntries = append(ec.orderedEntries, execEntry{cId: cId, result: e})
200: }
201: 
202: func (ec *ExecCollection) AddAll(cId string, e []*ExecResult) {
203: 	ec.m.Lock()
204: 	defer ec.m.Unlock()
205: 	ec.execEntries[cId] = append(ec.execEntries[cId], e...)
206: 	for _, result := range e {
207: 		ec.orderedEntries = append(ec.orderedEntries, execEntry{cId: cId, result: result})
208: 	}
209: }
210: 
211: // Dump dumps the contents of ExecCollection as a string in one of the provided formats.
212: func (ec *ExecCollection) Dump(format string) (string, error) {
213: 	ec.m.RLock()
214: 	defer ec.m.RUnlock()
215: 	result := strings.Builder{}
216: 	switch format {
217: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
218: 		byteData, err := json.MarshalIndent(ec.execEntries, "", "  ")
219: 		if err != nil {
220: 			return "", err
221: 		}
222: 
223: 		result.Write(byteData)
224: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain:
225: 		printSep := false
226: 		for k, execResults := range ec.execEntries {
227: 			if len(execResults) == 0 {
228: 				// skip if there is no result
229: 				continue
230: 			}
231: 
232: 			if printSep {
233: 				result.WriteString("\n+++++++++++++++++++++++++++++\n\n")
234: 			}
235: 			// write header for entry
236: 			result.WriteString("Node: ")
237: 			result.WriteString(k)
238: 			result.WriteString("\n")
239: 			for _, er := range execResults {
240: 				// write entry
241: 				result.WriteString(er.String())
242: 			}
243: 			// starting second run, print sep
244: 			printSep = true
245: 		}
246: 	}
247: 	return result.String(), nil
248: }
249: 
250: // Log writes to the log execution results stored in ExecCollection.
251: // If execution result contains error, the error log facility is used,
252: // otherwise it is logged as INFO.
253: func (ec *ExecCollection) Log() {
254: 	ec.m.RLock()
255: 	defer ec.m.RUnlock()
256: 	for _, entry := range ec.orderedEntries {
257: 		switch {
258: 		case entry.result.GetReturnCode() != 0:
259: 			log.Error(
260: 				"Failed to execute command",
261: 				"command", entry.result.GetCmdString(),
262: 				"node", entry.cId,
263: 				"rc", entry.result.GetReturnCode(),
264: 				"stdout", entry.result.GetStdOutString(),
265: 				"stderr", entry.result.GetStdErrString(),
266: 			)
267: 		default:
268: 			log.Info(
269: 				"Executed command",
270: 				"node", entry.cId,
271: 				"command", entry.result.GetCmdString(),
272: 				"stdout", entry.result.GetStdOutString(),
273: 			)
274: 		}
275: 	}
276: }

(End of file - total 276 lines)
